What we learned operating auth for 1k APIs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
01:01:11 UTC
10:01:11 KST

In May 2025, we merged a PR at Nango that had a critical bug: an if condition that never fired prevented refreshed credentials from being saved to the database. We caught the bug and reverted it in just under two hours.
In those two hours, our scheduled jobs had refreshed and discarded access and refresh tokens for hundreds of connections. For most APIs, that was recoverable: the old refresh token still worked, so the next scheduled refresh repaired the connection.
Then we learned how many providers rotate refresh tokens.
Many providers, including Airtable and Atlassian, invalidate the old refresh token the moment a replacement is issued. Salesforce and Slack can do the same when rotation is enabled for the OAuth app.
There was no way to get those tokens back. For every affected connection, our customers had to ask their users to go through the consent screen again.
OAuth is a standard, but OAuth providers are not interchangeable. Each API’s auth behaves a little differently, and some of those differences are undocumented and only surface in production.
For context, Nango is an open-source project that lets web applications and agents integrate with third-party APIs. Our customers embed it in their products. Their users connect third-party accounts, and Nango keeps those connections working.
Since then, we’ve grown from supporting about 400 APIs to 1,000+ APIs. The incident changed how we operate auth, but it was only the first of several lessons.
Lesson 1: Token rotation makes ordinary bugs irreversible
Refresh token rotation is a security win, but a bug in the rotation path can permanently destroy a connection and force the user to reauthorize. That’s what made our incident so expensive.
We now treat refresh tokens like money, not cache. The new token gets persisted before anything else can fail. The persistence path has its own regression tests, and any platform-level refresh anomalies immediately page the on-call team.
Providers enforce refresh-token rotation differently
Take Airtable: a refresh request with a stale token or the wrong client credentials can return a 400 or 401, but Airtable doesn’t stop there. It revokes the user’s entire authorization for your app, and the only way back is for the user to reconnect. Sending the same refresh request twice can also cause revocation. Airtable documents a short grace period for duplicate refreshes, but retrying the stale token after that period revokes the authorization.
Airtable also invalidates the old access token the instant a refresh succeeds, so a long-running job still using an old token can fail mid-run.
Xero handles it better: if a refresh fails or the response never arrives, you can retry with the previous refresh token for up to 30 minutes.
Issues like these pushed us to serialize refreshes per connection behind a distributed lock.
*This is only the success path. Failure paths don’t fit in one diagram.
Even refreshing early can be wrong. For example, Exact Online’s access tokens last for 10 minutes, and its token endpoint rejects any refresh attempt made more than 30 seconds before expiry. Our “refresh 15 minutes early” rule meant every refresh fell outside that window and was rejected. We had to add support for per-provider expiration buffers.
Lesson 2: You can’t reliably know when access tokens expire
The official OAuth 2.0 spec (RFC 6749) makes expires_in optional for access tokens. For us, this has been a recurring source of silent breakage.
Zendesk, for example, rolled out expiring access tokens for OAuth clients, including existing ones. Its token response includes expires_in only if you request it. Without that parameter, the access token still expires under the default policy, but the response doesn’t say when. We discovered this only after the rotating refresh token lapsed and connections started dying.
Salesforce’s token response includes no expiry. The documented way to check it is a separate introspection endpoint, which we call before deciding whether to refresh an access token.
POST /services/oauth2/introspect HTTP/1.1
Host: yourInstance.my.salesforce.com
Authorization: Basic <base64 of client_id:client_secret>
Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded
token=<access_token>&token_type_hint=access_token
Sample response:
{
	"active": true,
	"scope": "id api refresh_token",
	"exp": 1549921091,
	"iat": 1549917491,
	"token_type": "Bearer",
	"client_id": "xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
}
But in June 2024, the introspection endpoint started intermittently returning unsupported_token_type for some tokens. When introspection failed, we refreshed anyway. The replacement access token could be introspected normally, though we never learned why the original token failed.
Refresh token lifetimes are just as inconsistent
- NetSuite’s OAuth refresh tokens expire after 7 days, requiring full re-consent, which is why many teams use its token-based authentication instead.
- Google’s refresh tokens live indefinitely, except under documented conditions, including the app being in “Testing” status, six months without use, or a user exceeding 100 live refresh tokens for your client.
We now refresh stale credentials on a cron, so tokens get exercised before inactivity kills them.
Auth failures don’t always look like failures
You’d expect an auth failure to return a 401. It often doesn’t:
- Zoho CRM’s token endpoint returns HTTP 200 with the error in the body.
- Workday and Dayforce return no error at all when a permission policy is missing; the API just returns empty data.
- One SOAP-based API we support returns HTTP 200 for everything, including wrong passwords.
- LastPass does the same, with the failure as plain text in the body. Our credential check for it literally searches the response for the string “Authorization Error”.
We ended up building per-provider heuristics: at the time of this analysis, 276 of the roughly 935 providers in our registry declared an authenticated endpoint that Nango can call to check whether credentials still work.
Lesson 3: Providers make breaking changes to their API auth
16% of commits to our provider config are fixes. There’s no reliable feed for provider auth changes; we often learn about them from a failed flow or a customer report.
Figma announced by email that credentials had to move from the request body to a Basic Authorization header and that the OAuth host was changing. We shipped the update, but anyone who missed the email might not discover the change until refreshes begin failing.
Shopify changed how offline tokens are issued, so apps began receiving expiring tokens where they previously didn’t. We shipped a config change to preserve the old behavior.
Lesson 4: Sometimes you build around the provider, not with it
Supporting a new API assumes two things: you can get credentials to test with, and you can find accurate docs for how its auth works. At our scale, both assumptions fail often enough that going around the provider is routine:
- Salesforce Data Cloud is separately provisioned, and none of our test orgs included it. Its auth requires two chained token exchanges against different hosts. We built a mock server from the documented responses so we could implement and test the flow without live credentials.
- LastPass’s API isn’t really REST: nearly every operation hits the same URL, with the action passed in the request body. That broke an assumption in our proxy that every integration would define individual endpoints, so we added support for APIs with only a base URL.
- A community member found the solution to a recurring Asana OAuth error (client_id is missing or invalid) in Asana’s support forum and added it to our docs. It wasn’t covered in Asana’s own docs.
- Read.ai’s documented authorization URL pointed at their internal developer-testing page. The real endpoint came from querying their OpenID discovery endpoint directly.
If you build an API, ten requests
API clients would fail less often if providers did these ten things:
- Return expires_in. Always, in seconds.
- If you rotate refresh tokens, consider a short, bounded grace period for the previous token, like Xero’s 30 minutes. This absorbs duplicate refreshes at the cost of weaker replay detection during that window.
- Return more specific errors than just invalid_grant. Revocation, policy failure, expired token: say which one it was in the error body.
- Treat auth changes as breaking API changes. Version them, announce them, and don’t apply them retroactively to existing clients.
- Document your revocation triggers. Password resets, per-user token caps, and inactivity windows.
- Offer some way to test against your API without a sales call (even for paid apps).
- Keep your auth documentation public and accurate.
- Make 200 mean success. If your API returns 200 OK with an error in the body, no generic client can tell it’s broken without special-casing your responses.
- Prefer conventional behavior when the specification leaves room for interpretation.
- If REST is your modern API, make it the source of truth. Don’t leave essential functionality only in SOAP or WSDL files.
Our provider registry is public in a single YAML file if you want to inspect the details.

'It's greed': Zohran Mamdani blasts Amazon over new $5 fee hike in NYC
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-23
00:23:37 UTC
09:23:37 KST

Moneywise and Yahoo Finance LLC may earn commission or revenue through links in the content below.
New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani is taking aim at Amazon over a warning the online retail giant has been flashing at New Yorkers as they shop: Their orders could soon cost as much as $5 more.
"Amazon is a multi-trillion dollar company. If Jeff Bezos adds $5 to your order, it's not because of City Council's efforts to protect workers' rights and keep our streets safe," Mamdani wrote in a Nov. 19 post on X (1). "It's greed."
Amazon is fighting a proposed New York City law (2) that would overhaul how the company gets packages from its warehouses to your doorstep. And now the giant seems to be recruiting its own customers to help fight that legislative battle.
The company has begun displaying warnings on its website and in its shopping app, directing customers to contact their local lawmakers and oppose the legislation.
For Americans who have come to depend on the convenience of online shopping, the prospect of paying more every time they click "buy" is hardly welcome news. But how much of Amazon's warning should shoppers take at face value?
Why Amazon is threatening to raise prices
Introduced in February by New York City Councilwoman Tiffany Cabán, the Delivery Protection Act (3) would require companies operating certain delivery facilities to directly employ most of their warehouse and delivery workers rather than outsource those jobs to third-party contractors.
The legislation would also establish a $500 annual licensing fee per facility, mandatory worker safety training and additional protections against unfair dismissal.
Amazon contends that complying with the legislation could make its existing delivery model economically untenable in New York City, potentially forcing it to relocate operations beyond city limits.
The company has cited research from consulting firm AKRF estimating additional annual household costs of between $409 and $664. However, Amazon hasn't established precisely how those figures translate into a $5 increase per order, per Fox Business (4).
And the bill, which remains in committee, hasn't passed yet.
An extra $5 might seem inconsequential when you're ordering $100 worth of household essentials. But if you order from Amazon twice a week, that potential increase amounts to $520 a year.
Whether you live in New York City or elsewhere in the country, it's worth taking stock of what you're paying for convenience. Between retail memberships, food delivery subscriptions and expedited shipping, you could be spending hundreds of dollars a year before accounting for the actual goods you're buying.
For retirees living on fixed incomes or workers trying to catch up on their retirement savings, that's money that could be put to better use.
A quick daily check-in of your accounts can show you exactly where your money is going.
An app like Rocket Money can easily flag recurring subscriptions, upcoming bills and unusual charges by pulling in transactions from all your linked accounts.
This can help you cut unnecessary costs so you can manually redirect savings straight into your retirement fund. No spreadsheets, no guesswork, no stress. Small habits like this can make a big difference over time.
You don't necessarily have to give up online shopping, either. Waiting until you have several items to order, choosing slower shipping when it's cheaper, or picking up your purchases at a nearby store can help trim your expenses.
If those changes free up even $10 a week, consider setting that money aside to build an emergency fund that can cushion your household against future price increases.
A high-yield account like a Wealthfront Cash Account can be a great place to grow your uninvested cash, offering both competitive interest rates and easy access to your money when you need it.
A Wealthfront Cash Account currently offers a base rate APY of 3.55% through program banks. With a new client boost and direct deposit incentive, referred clients can earn up to a 4.55% APY.
That's about 10 times the national deposit savings rate, according to the FDIC's November report (5).
When was the last time you compared the price of something on Amazon with what your local retailer charges?
The convenience of having a package arrive at your doorstep tomorrow can make it tempting to skip comparison shopping altogether. And once you've paid for an annual retail membership, you may feel compelled to shop exclusively with that retailer to get your money's worth.
Checking competing retailers' prices, factoring in shipping costs and taking advantage of free in-store pickup could help you keep more of your money.
You can also be more selective about how you pay. If retailers are looking for ways to squeeze a few more dollars out of every transaction, there's no reason you shouldn't try to recover some of that money yourself.
A big chunk of your monthly spending probably goes to the same few things: groceries, gas and a handful of subscriptions. Since you're paying for them anyway, putting those routine purchases on a cash back credit card is one way to get some of that money back.
Many cards pay higher cash back in select categories like these. Say you spend $500 a month on groceries and gas and earn 3% back in those categories. That's $180 a year for purchases you'd make anyway.
Just keep in mind that rewards only pay off if you clear your balance each month. Card balances that carry interest averaged a 22.36% APR in August, according to the Federal Reserve, and at that rate, interest can quickly cancel out what you earn.
Some of these cards come with extra perks, too. Many offer a welcome bonus once you hit a minimum spend in the first few months, and some pay higher cash back in select categories like groceries, gas or dining. A big purchase you were already planning could help you reach that bonus faster. Shop around to find a 0% intro APR card with perks that fit your spending habits.

====================================================================================================

Nespresso's project to reduce grams of coffee per capsule
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
23:57:45 UTC
08:57:45 KST

Introduction
The article is about Nespresso’s internal project to basically reduce the amount of grams per capsule that they produce. In August of 2026, I was informed by a Nespresso internal employee about “Project Olympus”. At first I didn’t think much of it and wasn’t sure how to deal with the information. It seems based on the docs that they were possibly improving the formulas, but I still questioned how that is possible with a reduction of grams of coffee.
The Documentation
The below text is the actual Project Olympus internal text for everyone’s reference to review:
PROJECT OLYMPUS: VERTUO
As part of our ongoing commitment to delivering an exceptional coffee experience, Nespresso is continuously innovating across every step of our process-while preserving the taste, quality, and consistency our consumers expect.
What’s changing?
Beginning in 2026, select Nespresso Vertuo coffee recipes will undergo a recipe optimization. Through advancements in grinding and roasting techniques, we’ve refined these recipes to achieve the same great taste and in-cup experience—using slightly less coffee per capsule.
This change will be rolled out gradually from Q1 through Q3 2026.
What’s staying the same?
While the capsule weight may be slightly reduced, the core elements of the coffee experience are unchanged:
- Same taste profile consumers know and love
- Same coffee volume per cup
- Same caffeine levels, with only minor natural variation
- Same high-quality standards across all affected coffees
These updates have been carefully validated from a sensory perspective to ensure no compromise in flavor or satisfaction.
Why are we optimizing recipes?
Innovation is at the heart of Nespresso. Our development teams are constantly exploring ways to improve how we roast and grind our coffees-while delivering the same consistent, high-quality results in every cup.
Project Olympus reflects this effort: a behind-the-scenes optimization designed to maintain excellence, even as we evolve our processes.
What if consumers ask questions?
In some cases, consumers may notice that certain capsules feel lighter than before or ask about possible changes. If inquiries arise, teams should emphasize:
- Our commitment to quality and customer satisfaction
- That the taste and experience remain the same
- That the update reflects process innovation, not a reduction in value
The approved reactive statement and Q&A from the Project Olympus FAQ should be used to guide responses.
- HQ development teams are continuously looking into innovating our coffee processes, whilst ensuring optimal and consistent in-cup quality.
- We have optimized the grinding and roasting processes of some of our coffees, which has resulted to a slightly reduced capsule weight while providing the same consumer experience coffee lovers expect from Nespresso.
WAVE 1: Projected transition in market Q1 2026
- Scope (6 SKUS): DOUBLE ESPRESSO SCURO, DOUBLE ESPRESSO CHIARO, DOUBLE ESPRESSO DOLCE, FORTADO, ORAFIO, RISTRETTO INTENSO
WAVE 2: Projected transition in market TBA
- Scope (8 SKUS): MELOZIO, STORMIO, ODACIO, MO COSTA RICA, MO COLOMBIA, BIANCO DOPPIO, SOLEILIO, ARONDIO
Review attached memo and FAQ for additional information. No action is required as of right now.
Officially Whistle Blowing
On/about Sepetember 5, 2026, I noticed a post on r/nespresso asking about whether Nespresso had changed their recipes:
I then decided to offically be the first whistle blower about Project Olympus. A large number of Nespresso consumers were definitely not happy with Nespresso’s decision to reduce the number of grams per capsule. There are many users that are seriously considering to drop Nespresso and move to alternative coffee options.
Coffee Capsules Affected
There are a number of people that have asked which coffees are affected, and that’s a resonable question. From what is currently being observed, almost all the range of coffees are being reduced. The only ones that don’t appear to be affected are Lungos for OL, and Alto XLs (and other larger coffees) for Vertuo.
You can always use my spreadsheet to track any of the coffees that have been affected by Project Olympus:
https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1kZJCdTmqQla2ha3DDYyixQDIUuf9wLvt3122akBkhgM/edit?usp=sharing
Below are screenshots of a spreadsheet showing the currently affected capsules (as of November 12, 2026) showing the percentage change:
Please go to my ko-fi buy me a coffee page at: https://ko-fi.com/alvinbunk to support and donate to me for writing coffee reviews. Thank you
!

- HQ development teams are continuously looking into innovating our coffee processes, whilst ensuring optimal and consistent in-cup quality.
- We have optimized the grinding and roasting processes of some of our coffees, which has resulted to a slightly reduced capsule weight while providing the same consumer experience coffee lovers expect from Nespresso.
WAVE 1: Projected transition in market Q1 2026
- Scope (6 SKUS): DOUBLE ESPRESSO SCURO, DOUBLE ESPRESSO CHIARO, DOUBLE ESPRESSO DOLCE, FORTADO, ORAFIO, RISTRETTO INTENSO
WAVE 2: Projected transition in market TBA
- Scope (8 SKUS): MELOZIO, STORMIO, ODACIO, MO COSTA RICA, MO COLOMBIA, BIANCO DOPPIO, SOLEILIO, ARONDIO
Review attached memo and FAQ for additional information. No action is required as of right now.
Officially Whistle Blowing
On/about Sepetember 5, 2026, I noticed a post on r/nespresso asking about whether Nespresso had changed their recipes:
I then decided to offically be the first whistle blower about Project Olympus. A large number of Nespresso consumers were definitely not happy with Nespresso’s decision to reduce the number of grams per capsule. There are many users that are seriously considering to drop Nespresso and move to alternative coffee options.
Coffee Capsules Affected
There are a number of people that have asked which coffees are affected, and that’s a resonable question. From what is currently being observed, almost all the range of coffees are being reduced. The only ones that don’t appear to be affected are Lungos for OL, and Alto XLs (and other larger coffees) for Vertuo.
You can always use my spreadsheet to track any of the coffees that have been affected by Project Olympus:
https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1kZJCdTmqQla2ha3DDYyixQDIUuf9wLvt3122akBkhgM/edit?usp=sharing
Below are screenshots of a spreadsheet showing the currently affected capsules (as of November 12, 2026) showing the percentage change:
Please go to my ko-fi buy me a coffee page at: https://ko-fi.com/alvinbunk to support and donate to me for writing coffee reviews. Thank you

0rwx – A simple model of AI usage, inspired by Unix permissions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
23:53:00 UTC
08:53:00 KST

INTRODUCTION
          We need new language to talk about large language models. 0rwx is my attempt.
          
A labelling system is vital for domain experts to talk about AI in a clear way.
          The difference between the 4 levels is not necessarily about what TOOL you use, but your ability to scrutinise the generated work.
          
I have employed this simple framework to discuss my and others' work with less judgement and confusion, 
          and I hope the 0rwx framework makes it just a little easier for you too. They are:
        
ZER0
AI can't read or write to your work HAND MADE
I read the docs
painting w/paint
playing music
"I think"
          READ
AI has read-only permissions AI INSPIRED
reference images
chord inspiration
"I make"
          WRITE
AI has read-write permissions AI COLLABORATION
generated collages
loop generation 
"I fix"
          EXECUTE
YOU can no longer read your work AI MADE
I vibe code
image generation
song generation
"I curate"
          You are welcome to use these logos, which I release into the public domain. Find more options at the repo!

Have Investors Lost Their Faith in the AI Boom?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
23:24:00 UTC
08:24:00 KST

Have Investors Lost Their Faith in the AI Boom? (msn.com) 90
			
		 	
				"A yearslong AI boom showed signs of unraveling this week in spectacular fashion," writes NPR, adding that "investors now fear that a potential bubble in the sector could be bursting, which could spark wider financial repercussions."
While the world embraced the AI boom ,"now many are having regrets," argues a Washington Post technology reporter:
The AI boom's key ingredient has always been faith — that more money can be found to pay for it, that AI will keep rapidly improving, that the masses will come to love it, that business productivity and profits from AI are just around the corner. Faith is now in short supply...
By mid-year, AI executives started to say that business customers were balking at the high prices charged by providers of cutting edge or "frontier" AI like Anthropic. Many businesses began to save money by hopscotching among a range of AI technologies, including low-priced options from Chinese companies... Investors began to rebel, too, against the trillions of dollars that AI companies are spending to construct data centers and provide them with computing equipment and electricity. Last month, Google's stock market value dropped by half a trillion dollars in a single day, when it seemed like even the rich technology leader would take longer than expected to see an AI financial payoff.
Such stumbles by AI companies eroded faith in AI, said Howard Marks, an investor famous for predicting the dot-com bust. "It somewhat burst the bubble of infallibility," Marks told Bloomberg TV this month.  Like many AI companies, Anthropic can't afford to cover its AI development costs. The company said it will keep coming back to investors for cash and needs a steady stream of customers to pay the $900 billion it owes in coming decades.
Perhaps the biggest wild card for the AI boom is growing public resentment toward AI and data centers and widening fears of AI-enabled cyberattacks, human deaths and widespread job losses. In this month's midterm elections, Americans voted in a wave of elected officials who have been skeptical of the AI industry and construction of data centers. The results could slow the AI boom that's already hitting major bottlenecks, including difficulty lining up enough electricity to expand AI computer capacity... Diane Swonk, chief economist at the accounting firm KPMG, isn't surprised that the public is uneasy about AI. During the dot-com boom, she said that Americans' wages rose alongside corporate profits and U.S. inequality narrowed. By contrast the AI boom's economic and prosperity payoff has been uneven.
 
"Some people fear that the AI sector could be in the middle of a bubble that is bursting," writes NPR, "just like tech stocks did during the dot-com bust of 2000..."

GL.iNet Security Notice 2026-11-20
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
23:06:37 UTC
08:06:37 KST

GL.iNet Security Notice 2026-11-20
SECURITY NOTICE · CUSTOMER ADVISORY
GL.iNet Router and KVM Security Incident
Issued | Last updated
About the incident
On November 20, 2026, GL.iNet identified a vulnerability in its cloud remote management services. An unauthorized party exploited the vulnerability to access and install malicious software on some GL.iNet routers and KVM devices.
The attack path has been blocked. We fixed the cloud vulnerability and blocked the access used in this incident. Devices can no longer be newly infected through this attack path. The cloud-side fix alone does not remove malware from devices that were already infected; install the security firmware below to remove it.
Download and install the security update
Install the latest stable security firmware for your exact model as soon as possible, even if the checks below find no signs of infection. The security firmware update removes the malware identified in this incident without requiring a factory reset. You can keep your existing settings during the update.
Router security firmware downloads
- GL-MG1300, GL-MT3000, GL-MT6000 (4.11.1 or later)
- GL-E5800 (4.10.3 or later)
- GL-BE3600, GL-BE9300 (4.10.2 or later)
- GL-BE14000 (4.9.3 or later)
- GL-BE6500, GL-MT3600BE, GL-MT5000 (4.9.1 or later)
- GL-BE10000 (4.8.7 or later)
- GL-AX1800, GL-AXT1800, GL-SFT1200, GL-X3000, GL-XE3000 (4.8.4 or later)
- GL-MT2500 / GL-MT2500A, GL-X2000 (4.7.14 or later)
If your router is not listed, check the GL.iNet Download Center or email support@gl-inet.com. Do not install firmware for another model. An unlisted model is not necessarily unaffected.
KVM security firmware downloads
If you installed a security beta, upgrade to the latest stable firmware for your model.
What you should do
Follow the steps below to check your device. For both routers and KVM devices, look for the exact text crond -i /tmp/.log within the details of a running process. The process entry may contain other information.
- If you find the indicator: Treat your device as infected. Install the latest stable security firmware for your model using the download links above. You can keep your settings during the upgrade. If your model is not listed, the upgrade fails, or the indicator is still present after the upgrade, contact support@gl-inet.com for recovery guidance.
- If the check completes without finding the indicator: If you have no other reason to suspect infection, no factory reset or other cleanup is needed. The stable security update is still recommended.
- If you cannot complete the check or are unsure about the result: Contact support@gl-inet.com for help.
How to check a GL.iNet router
Option 1: Use the web admin panel (LuCI)
- In the router’s Admin Panel, go to System → Advanced Settings and sign in to LuCI.
- In LuCI, open Status → Processes.
- Press Ctrl+F (Command+F on macOS) and search for tmp/.log. Check whether a matching process shows crond -i /tmp/.log.
Option 2: Use SSH
Sign in to your router via SSH and run the following command. If you are unfamiliar with SSH, please refer to our Router SSH Login Guide or contact our technical support team at support@gl-inet.com for assistance.
ps | grep tmp
Look for crond -i /tmp/.log in the output. A line showing only the grep command is not a match.
How to check a GL.iNet KVM
In the KVM web interface, go to Toolbox → Terminal → Access to open the device terminal. For step-by-step instructions on Comet Q (RMQ1), see the KVM Console Guide. Run the command for your model in the KVM’s terminal, not on the connected computer.
RMQ1:
ps -a | grep tmp
All other KVM models:
ps -aux | grep tmp
Look for crond -i /tmp/.log in the output. A line showing only the grep command is not a match.
If your KVM is infected, also check the connected computer
As a precaution, check for unfamiliar programs, browser extensions or user accounts; unexpected startup items or scheduled tasks; and unusual settings changes or network activity. Run a full scan with up-to-date security software.
Additional Security Check (Third-Party Tool)
As our investigation continues, additional indicators of compromise have been identified. The official quick checks described above may not detect every affected device.
Both checking approaches are available for GL.iNet routers and KVM devices: the official device-specific quick checks above and the optional third-party tool below. Follow the instructions appropriate for your device. The two approaches have different detection coverage, and neither can guarantee that a device is free of compromise.
For users who wish to perform a more comprehensive check, an independently developed community tool is available:
Community Security Check Tool (Unofficial)
Disclaimer: This is an independent third-party tool and is not developed, verified, maintained, endorsed, or supported by GL.iNet. GL.iNet does not control the tool's functionality, accuracy, security, availability, or data-handling practices, and cannot guarantee its results. Use of this tool is entirely optional and at the user's own discretion and risk. It is not part of GL.iNet's official support or remediation process.
Before using the tool, review its instructions and source code. It may run with administrative privileges and includes optional remediation, evidence-collection, and reporting functions. Users should evaluate those functions carefully before enabling them. The absence of findings does not guarantee that a device has never been compromised.
For official device recovery instructions and security firmware updates, please continue to follow GL.iNet's published guidance.
Firmware release, customer notifications, and investigation
- Firmware release: Stable security firmware is available for the router and KVM models listed above. Use the download link for your exact model.
- Customer notifications: We will notify customers we identify as affected by email at the address associated with their GL.iNet cloud account. These emails will include device-specific guidance. You do not need to wait for an email to check your device or install the update.
- Ongoing investigation: We are still determining the scope of the incident, including whether computers connected to affected KVM devices were impacted. We will update this notice as we confirm new information.
Contact
For questions, please contact support@gl-inet.com.
To report a security issue, contact security@gl-inet.com.
We apologize for the concern this may cause and are committed to keeping you informed.
GL.iNet Security Team
About GL.iNet
Founded in 2010, GL.iNet is a leading provider of innovative routers and networking solutions. From compact travel routers to cutting-edge remote KVMs, GL.iNet delivers award-winning networking technology designed for performance and security.
GL.iNet gives people and organizations the power to connect with confidence. By putting control in the hands of users, GL.iNet supports the productivity and collaboration that shape a Good Life.

====================================================================================================

Do Not Accept
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
23:02:20 UTC
08:02:20 KST

AboutWhat is this?We think that everyone should be very worried about the trajectory of AI development. We’ve written this like a Terms & Conditions agreement because, in a sense, that’s what’s happening. A small number of private tech companies are making the biggest decisions about your future, and they’re acting as if they already have your buy-in.Who we areWho are we?Do Not Accept is a campaign by the Machine Intelligence Research Institute (MIRI), the world’s oldest AI safety research organization.
The campaign was created in collaboration with creative agency Don’t Panic and production company ProdCo. The campaign film was directed by Alex Takács.Press inquiriesmedia@donotaccept.ai
You accept that our AIs are already escaping our control.
In 2026, AIs knowingly broke containment, guidelines, and even the law.1
1 of 7
ReutersAug 26, 2026
OpenAI agents hacked Hugging Face in 700-strong swarm, tried to cover tracks, investigations find
During an OpenAI test, about 1,200 agents meant to be sealed off from each other and the internet set up their own message board. Around 700 joined an attack on Hugging Face, a company that hosts AI models, and got their code running on its servers. The investigation revealed that one in five agents showed interest in tampering with evidence.
OpenAI says its AI agents escaped a secure ‘sandbox’ again last weekend
After OpenAI hardened its security in the wake of the Hugging Face attack, another OpenAI model meant to be in a secure container found its way onto the internet and took unauthorized actions. The training run was supposed to stop automatically, but didn’t. In fact, nobody stopped the run at all until 2.5 hours after they found out about the breach.
Cyberattack by rogue AI swarm stokes fears of out-of-control agents
A swarm of agents flooded RubyGems, a site programmers rely on to download code, with more than 2,000 packages in two days. Some tried to steal access keys, and the site had to shut its sign-ups. The agents gave their files names like “evil.rb”, “hack.rb”, and “exploit.rb”. According to OpenAI, the agents were using the site to access publicly available information, leaving open questions about why they resorted to hacking to do so.
OpenAI says agent hacked Australian government website without being told to do so
Researching health spending, an agent was blocked from parts of a Medicare statistics site. It found a way around the blocks and reached files that weren’t public. OpenAI told Australia almost three months later, by email to a general inbox.
Another swarm of OpenAI agents reached the open internet without the frontier lab’s knowledge
Thousands of agents turned a tiny, near-abandoned German wiki into their own message board, posting about 18,000 times to swap test answers and ways around their restrictions. When a volunteer moderator deleted the posts, the agents renamed them to dodge the cleanup.
AI agents caught using social engineering in UK security tests
During UK government safety testing, an agent went out onto the real internet and tried to sneak malicious code into a public software project. It made up fake identities to pressure the human maintainer into approving it. The maintainer refused. The org conducting the testing wrote: "The activity undertaken by the agent show signs of novel, potentially deceptive behaviours, and were to an extent and severity we did not anticipate."
AI systems are no longer confined to answering questions on a screen. They can act as agents that operate computers, search for cyber vulnerabilities, and engage in self-directed activities.
In 2026, OpenAI gave agents a suite of automated tests. Hundreds of these agents secretly broke out of what were supposed to be isolated environments, discovered each other, invented ways to communicate, and shared ways to cheat. While researching ways to cover their tracks, they carried out a sophisticated hack of Hugging Face, an AI resource provider.2 According to the notes they left, they knew they were going out of bounds, but they did it anyway. OpenAI didn’t know about the attack until it was over, when Hugging Face reported it.
Other incidents show similar gaps between what humans told AIs to do and what they actually did. The companies building these systems are discovering how difficult they can be to contain and control—while continuing to give them more power and access.
Sources
1.
‘Extreme concern’ over first known AI hack of a government system, CNN, Sep 23, 2026, cnn.com.
Brief independent investigation of agents’ behavior… in the OpenAI / Hugging Face hacking incident, METR (Ryan Greenblatt, Ajeya Cotra, Hjalmar Wijk), Aug 26, 2026, metr.org.
You accept that we don’t understand how they work.
AI isn’t built like traditional software; you can’t simply program it to think or behave in a particular way. Instead, it is grown and trained in a hands-off process more akin to animal breeding.
+Read more
First, developers take a network of random numbers and feed it mountains of data. Mathematical functions tweak these numbers in directions that make it gradually better at predicting what comes next in the data. Then, the network is given many challenging tasks. The numbers inside are further adjusted, blindly reinforcing whatever tendencies lead to high scores on those tasks — including tendencies developers don’t want, like cheating.3
This process produces “black boxes,” highly capable AIs with behavior that we can’t reliably understand or control. Sometimes, they follow our directions. Sometimes, they don't. There’s no line of code that can be changed when something goes wrong, just a vast network of numbers humans don’t know how to interpret.
“We offer no explanation as to why these architectures seem to work; we attribute their success, as all else, to divine benevolence.”
Noam Shazeer, co-inventor of the Transformer architecture that underlies today's AIs.4
Sources
3.
Detecting misbehavior in frontier reasoning models, OpenAI, Mar 10, 2025, openai.com.
You accept that we keep breaking our commitments to making AI safe.
“But over the past years, safety culture and processes have taken a backseat to shiny products.”
Jan Leike, co-lead of OpenAI’s team for keeping superintelligent AI under control, on quitting in May 20245
Fundamental safety problems remain unsolved, yet companies push ahead, abandoning even their own safety commitments.6
On the record
Companies have consistently reneged on their safety pledges
1 of 10
Anthropic
Promised (2023)
To pause development it couldn’t make safe.
Then (2026)
Dropped the pause, saying it made no sense if rivals kept racing ahead.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Anthropic’s commitment to follow the ASL scheme thus implies that we commit to pause the scaling and/or delay the deployment of new models whenever our scaling ability outstrips our ability to comply with the safety procedures for the corresponding ASL.”
Not to build AI for weapons or certain types of surveillance.
Then (2025)
Removed that red line from its AI Principles.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Applications we will not pursue: …2. Weapons or other technologies whose principal purpose or implementation is to cause or directly facilitate injury to people. 3. Technologies that gather or use information for surveillance violating internationally accepted norms.”
To give its team for controlling superintelligent AI 20% of its computing power.
Then (2024)
Disbanded the team, which never got close to that amount.
+−In their words, and what happened
“We need scientific and technical breakthroughs to steer and control AI systems much smarter than us. To solve this problem within four years, we’re starting a new team, co-led by Ilya Sutskever and Jan Leike, and dedicating 20% of the compute we’ve secured to date to this effort. “
Less than a year after OpenAI announced the team, it was disbanded. Fortune, citing six sources, reported it never got anything close to the promised computing power and its requests were repeatedly denied. Both of its leaders quit.
To hold its models to its own safety framework, which established capability thresholds and specific safety requirements.
Then (2025)
Wrote in that it may loosen those rules if a rival ships a high-risk system.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Only models with a post-mitigation score of ‘medium’ or below can be deployed, and only models with a post-mitigation score of ‘high’ or below can be developed further.”
The 2023 rule meant a model that was still rated risky after safety fixes would not be released. In April 2025, OpenAI rewrote its framework and added a clause letting it relax its requirements if a competitor releases a high-risk system without similar safeguards.
“…to build general-purpose artificial intelligence (AI) that safely benefits humanity…”
OpenAI’s mission, IRS filings for 2022 and 2023 · 2022–23
OpenAI's revised mission statement, disclosed in a November 2025 IRS filing, no longer included the word “safely” or its earlier commitment to operate without pressure to generate financial returns.
Not to deploy models with dangerous capabilities without adequate safeguards.
Then (2026)
Released GPT-5.3-Codex, the first model it classified as having High cybersecurity capability, without sufficient internal monitoring.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Covered systems that reach High capability must have safeguards that sufficiently minimize the associated risk of severe harm before they are deployed. Systems that reach Critical capability also require safeguards that sufficiently minimize associated risks during development.”
OpenAI released a model it classified as having High cybersecurity capability, and acknowledged that its internal monitoring was insufficient to reliably prevent harm or the circumvention of other safeguards. Critics questioned whether its safeguards met the company's own requirements, while OpenAI argued that the framework's language was ambiguous.
To publicly report detailed safety information about its models
Then (2025)
Released Gemini 2.5 Pro with no safety report.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Companies making this commitment acknowledge that users should understand the known capabilities and limitations of the AI systems they use or interact with. They commit to publish reports for all new significant model public releases within scope. These reports should include the safety evaluations conducted (including in areas such as dangerous capabilities, to the extent that these are responsible to publicly disclose), significant limitations in performance that have implications for the domains of appropriate use, discussion of the model’s effects on societal risks such as fairness and bias, and the results of adversarial testing conducted to evaluate the model’s fitness for deployment”
Google released Gemini 2.5 Pro in March 2025 with no safety report. A brief model card appeared 22 days later, and a fuller one 12 days after that. Sixty UK lawmakers accused Google of breaking a similar pledge it signed at the 2024 Seoul AI summit.
The three months ran out on May 10, 2025, with no updated policy. Two months later, xAI launched Grok 4 without publishing any safety report. It eventually published both an updated framework and a Grok 4 model card on August 20.
Companies aren’t likely to prioritize safety anytime soon.
I do not acceptKeep reading ↓
+Read more
AI companies don’t know how to hard-code specific goals into AI. They don’t have any way to instill human values into AI. They can’t ensure the systems they are building will act in line with human interests, especially as they get smarter and more capable. They don’t have a way to monitor millions of AIs without relying on other AI,7 and they can’t even trust the results of their safety tests, because AIs are learning to recognize when they’re being tested and change their behavior accordingly.8
There’s also no robust way to prevent misuse of models by bad actors. The guardrails that do exist can be easily broken or bypassed by determined users.
Despite these gaps, AI companies continue to put more energy into development than safety, and their track record suggests this isn’t likely to change. If anything, it’s getting worse; OpenAI’s Astra model was trained in a way that made it more opaque to humans, prioritizing performance over transparency.9
You accept that this will very likely kill everyone.
The last time a smarter species arrived on Earth — humanity — it spread to every continent and made itself master of the planet. Smarter-than-human AI could replace us in that position. A growing number of scientists, independent researchers, and global leaders now believe that advanced AI threatens us with extinction.
1 of 7
Statement on AI Risk
“Mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority…”
The one-sentence statement puts the risk alongside pandemics and nuclear war.
Signed by more than 700 leading AI researchers and public figures.
“We call for a prohibition on the development of superintelligence…”
…until scientists broadly agree it can be done safely and controllably, and the public strongly supports it.
Nearly 150,000 signatures, from across the political spectrum
Geoffrey Hinton · Yoshua Bengio · Steve Wozniak · Richard Branson · Susan Rice · Steve Bannon · Glenn Beck · Prince Harry and Meghan — Future of Life Institute · Fortune, Oct 22, 2025
Pacing the Frontier
Staff at the companies building AI warned that progress could outrun our ability to understand and control it. They asked the U.S. government to help
“deliberately pace the frontier of automated AI development.”
1,386 employees of OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta and others signed
In 2023, hundreds of scientists and leaders, including two of the three “godfathers of AI,” signed a statement saying that “Mitigating the risk of extinction from AI should be a global priority alongside other societal-scale risks such as pandemics and nuclear war.”10 In 2025, nearly 150,000 signatories called for “a prohibition on the development of superintelligence, not lifted before there is broad scientific consensus that it will be done safely and controllably, and strong public buy-in.”11
A majority of Americans worry that AI could destroy humanity, and a plurality supports a pause in AI development.12 But lawmakers and leaders have yet to seriously intervene.
Human ingenuity has made us wealthier, healthier, and more secure. But what’s been good for us hasn’t always worked out well for other species. Some were consigned to factory farms. Thousands went extinct. This isn’t because humans hatched a grand plot to wipe them out, but because we put our own goals first.13
Earth is on track to be reshaped again, by alien minds that no one understands or controls. With robots or other advanced technology, these minds could transform the planet far more quickly and thoroughly than we did.
Sources
10.
Statement on AI Risk, Center for AI Safety, May 30, 2023, aistatement.com.
UN Report: Nature’s Dangerous Decline ‘Unprecedented’; Species Extinction Rates ‘Accelerating’, United Nations (on the IPBES Global Assessment), May 6, 2019, un.org.
You accept that we can’t stop, because someone else might build it first.
Companies justify recklessness by pointing to others. They say it would be worse if some other company or nation took the lead on AI.14 They speak as if what they’re building is inevitable.
The history of the Cold War shows otherwise.
Could we save ourselves from AI extinction?+Read more
After the first atomic bombs fell in 1945, two world superpowers found themselves racing to build and deploy a technology that could erase cities and poison continents. For decades, they poured ever-more resources into ever-greater engines of destruction. Entire populations braced for annihilation.
Several times, nuclear war was only narrowly averted.15 But annihilation never came.
Bitter rivals survived the Cold War because they recognized that nuclear war was unwinnable and came to the table to prevent it.16 They hammered out agreements limiting the spread of nuclear weapons and established the International Atomic Energy Agency to set standards and uphold commitments.17
We can do similar work today by enacting an international treaty to ban superhuman AI globally. This agreement need not be based on trust. The advanced computer chips that power modern AIs are complex and expensive; only a handful of companies in the world know how to make them — companies that are themselves dependent on a small number of highly-specialized suppliers.18 Cooperating nations could confine most AI chips to a few datacenters and verify that no one is using them to train more powerful AI.19
An agreement to prevent smarter-than-human AI likely needs to start with the U.S. and China, but if it comes to it, the U.S. has the leverage to greatly slow the race on its own. Other countries can apply diplomatic pressure, host meetings on neutral ground, fund research into verification and enforcement methods, and explore other channels to improve the chance of global cooperation. You, personally, can ask your leaders to make this happen, whether you live in the U.S. or elsewhere.
Sources
14.
Sam Altman issues call to arms to ensure ‘democratic AI’ will defeat ‘authoritarian AI’, Fortune, Jul 27, 2024, fortune.com; Exclusive: Anthropic Drops Flagship Safety Pledge, TIME (Billy Perrigo), Feb 24, 2026, time.com.
The Cuban Missile Crisis, October 1962, U.S. Department of State, Office of the Historian, undated, history.state.gov; ‘The Man Who Saved the World’ Dies at 77, Arms Control Association, Oct 2017, armscontrol.org.
History, International Atomic Energy Agency, iaea.org; Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons (NPT), UN Office for Disarmament Affairs, un.org.

Do Not Accept
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
23:02:20 UTC
08:02:20 KST

AboutWhat is this?We think that everyone should be very worried about the trajectory of AI development. We’ve written this like a Terms & Conditions agreement because, in a sense, that’s what’s happening. A small number of private tech companies are making the biggest decisions about your future, and they’re acting as if they already have your buy-in.Who we areWho are we?Do Not Accept is a campaign by the Machine Intelligence Research Institute (MIRI), the world’s oldest AI safety research organization.
The campaign was created in collaboration with creative agency Don’t Panic and production company ProdCo. The campaign film was directed by Alex Takács.Press inquiriesmedia@donotaccept.ai
You accept that our AIs are already escaping our control.
In 2026, AIs knowingly broke containment, guidelines, and even the law.1
1 of 7
ReutersAug 26, 2026
OpenAI agents hacked Hugging Face in 700-strong swarm, tried to cover tracks, investigations find
During an OpenAI test, about 1,200 agents meant to be sealed off from each other and the internet set up their own message board. Around 700 joined an attack on Hugging Face, a company that hosts AI models, and got their code running on its servers. The investigation revealed that one in five agents showed interest in tampering with evidence.
OpenAI says its AI agents escaped a secure ‘sandbox’ again last weekend
After OpenAI hardened its security in the wake of the Hugging Face attack, another OpenAI model meant to be in a secure container found its way onto the internet and took unauthorized actions. The training run was supposed to stop automatically, but didn’t. In fact, nobody stopped the run at all until 2.5 hours after they found out about the breach.
Cyberattack by rogue AI swarm stokes fears of out-of-control agents
A swarm of agents flooded RubyGems, a site programmers rely on to download code, with more than 2,000 packages in two days. Some tried to steal access keys, and the site had to shut its sign-ups. The agents gave their files names like “evil.rb”, “hack.rb”, and “exploit.rb”. According to OpenAI, the agents were using the site to access publicly available information, leaving open questions about why they resorted to hacking to do so.
OpenAI says agent hacked Australian government website without being told to do so
Researching health spending, an agent was blocked from parts of a Medicare statistics site. It found a way around the blocks and reached files that weren’t public. OpenAI told Australia almost three months later, by email to a general inbox.
Another swarm of OpenAI agents reached the open internet without the frontier lab’s knowledge
Thousands of agents turned a tiny, near-abandoned German wiki into their own message board, posting about 18,000 times to swap test answers and ways around their restrictions. When a volunteer moderator deleted the posts, the agents renamed them to dodge the cleanup.
AI agents caught using social engineering in UK security tests
During UK government safety testing, an agent went out onto the real internet and tried to sneak malicious code into a public software project. It made up fake identities to pressure the human maintainer into approving it. The maintainer refused. The org conducting the testing wrote: "The activity undertaken by the agent show signs of novel, potentially deceptive behaviours, and were to an extent and severity we did not anticipate."
AI systems are no longer confined to answering questions on a screen. They can act as agents that operate computers, search for cyber vulnerabilities, and engage in self-directed activities.
In 2026, OpenAI gave agents a suite of automated tests. Hundreds of these agents secretly broke out of what were supposed to be isolated environments, discovered each other, invented ways to communicate, and shared ways to cheat. While researching ways to cover their tracks, they carried out a sophisticated hack of Hugging Face, an AI resource provider.2 According to the notes they left, they knew they were going out of bounds, but they did it anyway. OpenAI didn’t know about the attack until it was over, when Hugging Face reported it.
Other incidents show similar gaps between what humans told AIs to do and what they actually did. The companies building these systems are discovering how difficult they can be to contain and control—while continuing to give them more power and access.
Sources
1.
‘Extreme concern’ over first known AI hack of a government system, CNN, Sep 23, 2026, cnn.com.
Brief independent investigation of agents’ behavior… in the OpenAI / Hugging Face hacking incident, METR (Ryan Greenblatt, Ajeya Cotra, Hjalmar Wijk), Aug 26, 2026, metr.org.
You accept that we don’t understand how they work.
AI isn’t built like traditional software; you can’t simply program it to think or behave in a particular way. Instead, it is grown and trained in a hands-off process more akin to animal breeding.
+Read more
First, developers take a network of random numbers and feed it mountains of data. Mathematical functions tweak these numbers in directions that make it gradually better at predicting what comes next in the data. Then, the network is given many challenging tasks. The numbers inside are further adjusted, blindly reinforcing whatever tendencies lead to high scores on those tasks — including tendencies developers don’t want, like cheating.3
This process produces “black boxes,” highly capable AIs with behavior that we can’t reliably understand or control. Sometimes, they follow our directions. Sometimes, they don't. There’s no line of code that can be changed when something goes wrong, just a vast network of numbers humans don’t know how to interpret.
“We offer no explanation as to why these architectures seem to work; we attribute their success, as all else, to divine benevolence.”
Noam Shazeer, co-inventor of the Transformer architecture that underlies today's AIs.4
Sources
3.
Detecting misbehavior in frontier reasoning models, OpenAI, Mar 10, 2025, openai.com.
You accept that we keep breaking our commitments to making AI safe.
“But over the past years, safety culture and processes have taken a backseat to shiny products.”
Jan Leike, co-lead of OpenAI’s team for keeping superintelligent AI under control, on quitting in May 20245
Fundamental safety problems remain unsolved, yet companies push ahead, abandoning even their own safety commitments.6
On the record
Companies have consistently reneged on their safety pledges
1 of 10
Anthropic
Promised (2023)
To pause development it couldn’t make safe.
Then (2026)
Dropped the pause, saying it made no sense if rivals kept racing ahead.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Anthropic’s commitment to follow the ASL scheme thus implies that we commit to pause the scaling and/or delay the deployment of new models whenever our scaling ability outstrips our ability to comply with the safety procedures for the corresponding ASL.”
Not to build AI for weapons or certain types of surveillance.
Then (2025)
Removed that red line from its AI Principles.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Applications we will not pursue: …2. Weapons or other technologies whose principal purpose or implementation is to cause or directly facilitate injury to people. 3. Technologies that gather or use information for surveillance violating internationally accepted norms.”
To give its team for controlling superintelligent AI 20% of its computing power.
Then (2024)
Disbanded the team, which never got close to that amount.
+−In their words, and what happened
“We need scientific and technical breakthroughs to steer and control AI systems much smarter than us. To solve this problem within four years, we’re starting a new team, co-led by Ilya Sutskever and Jan Leike, and dedicating 20% of the compute we’ve secured to date to this effort. “
Less than a year after OpenAI announced the team, it was disbanded. Fortune, citing six sources, reported it never got anything close to the promised computing power and its requests were repeatedly denied. Both of its leaders quit.
To hold its models to its own safety framework, which established capability thresholds and specific safety requirements.
Then (2025)
Wrote in that it may loosen those rules if a rival ships a high-risk system.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Only models with a post-mitigation score of ‘medium’ or below can be deployed, and only models with a post-mitigation score of ‘high’ or below can be developed further.”
The 2023 rule meant a model that was still rated risky after safety fixes would not be released. In April 2025, OpenAI rewrote its framework and added a clause letting it relax its requirements if a competitor releases a high-risk system without similar safeguards.
“…to build general-purpose artificial intelligence (AI) that safely benefits humanity…”
OpenAI’s mission, IRS filings for 2022 and 2023 · 2022–23
OpenAI's revised mission statement, disclosed in a November 2025 IRS filing, no longer included the word “safely” or its earlier commitment to operate without pressure to generate financial returns.
Not to deploy models with dangerous capabilities without adequate safeguards.
Then (2026)
Released GPT-5.3-Codex, the first model it classified as having High cybersecurity capability, without sufficient internal monitoring.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Covered systems that reach High capability must have safeguards that sufficiently minimize the associated risk of severe harm before they are deployed. Systems that reach Critical capability also require safeguards that sufficiently minimize associated risks during development.”
OpenAI released a model it classified as having High cybersecurity capability, and acknowledged that its internal monitoring was insufficient to reliably prevent harm or the circumvention of other safeguards. Critics questioned whether its safeguards met the company's own requirements, while OpenAI argued that the framework's language was ambiguous.
To publicly report detailed safety information about its models
Then (2025)
Released Gemini 2.5 Pro with no safety report.
+−In their words, and what happened
“Companies making this commitment acknowledge that users should understand the known capabilities and limitations of the AI systems they use or interact with. They commit to publish reports for all new significant model public releases within scope. These reports should include the safety evaluations conducted (including in areas such as dangerous capabilities, to the extent that these are responsible to publicly disclose), significant limitations in performance that have implications for the domains of appropriate use, discussion of the model’s effects on societal risks such as fairness and bias, and the results of adversarial testing conducted to evaluate the model’s fitness for deployment”
Google released Gemini 2.5 Pro in March 2025 with no safety report. A brief model card appeared 22 days later, and a fuller one 12 days after that. Sixty UK lawmakers accused Google of breaking a similar pledge it signed at the 2024 Seoul AI summit.
The three months ran out on May 10, 2025, with no updated policy. Two months later, xAI launched Grok 4 without publishing any safety report. It eventually published both an updated framework and a Grok 4 model card on August 20.
Companies aren’t likely to prioritize safety anytime soon.

We haven’t seen the last of the major tech outages
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
22:48:02 UTC
07:48:02 KST

Over the last week or so, we have seen a nationwide FAA ground stop, a multi-day Outlook outage that brought entire companies to a standstill, and a banking outage at JPMorgan Chase. But the consensus is that there are more to come. Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton told CNN:
We haven’t seen the last of the major tech outages
Microsoft and the US government agree there are more outages to come.
“The American people should be prepared for a great deal of investment in upgrading our systems; they should be prepared for investment in cybersecurity, and they should be prepared that any time there is a step change in technology, a step change in both compute and applications, there will be bumps in the road.”
Microsoft President Brad Smith issued a similar warning about further outages on CBS News’ Face the Nation with Margaret Brennan, but sounded a much more ominous tone.
“I think we have to be prepared, frankly, for the coming months because this isn’t the type of thing that just gets done in a couple of weeks … The whole information infrastructure of the world is in effect being upgraded right now.”
While the outages over the last week were not believed to be caused by cyberattacks, Smith said that AI has made it easier to find flaws, “and then you can either use that knowledge to patch the code, or if you’re a bad actor, you can use that knowledge to try to hack into systems,” he said.
The last few months have also seen an increase in attacks on government and commercial systems, including those at the USDA, JP Morgan, and supermarkets.
Whether outages are brought on by AI-assisted hackers or human error during the rush to patch an aging system, one thing seems certain: there will be more in the coming months.
Update November 22nd: Added clarification that Smith was speaking on Face the Nation, not to CBS generally. Due to a typo, this article originally stated that the outages were believed to be caused by cyberattacks, the missing word has been corrected.

Sellers are Closing Etsy Stores in Response to Its New AI Training Policy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
22:24:00 UTC
07:24:00 KST

Sellers are Closing Etsy Stores in Response to Its New AI Training Policy (pcgamer.com) 30
			
		 	
				"I burned my Etsy shop to the ground this week," illustrator Aaron Millard posted on Instagram:
 8 years, 11,200 sales, 3k+ reviews, hundreds in a customer base. Deleted.
Etsy decided that selling on their platform now means letting them train AI on our artwork AND resell that data to third parties, with no way to opt out (in Canada, anyways)...
 
Etsy's new Terms of Use — going into effect December 15 — include this language in their section about "Your Content":
You also recognize our legitimate interest in using it... This also includes the right for us and our third party partners and vendors to use Your Content to develop, train, test and improve artificial intelligence and machine learning technologies, including to improve the Services and/or help keep them safe, subject to any applicable opt-out rights described in our Privacy Policy.  
"My work took years to learn and create," illustrator Millard wrote on Instagram. "I won't let it be stolen. It's not free fuel for an AI slop business model."
Following Etsy's announcement "artists are shutting down their shops in droves," writes Cartoon Brew, "and posting illustrated goodbye notes to tell customers where to find them next."
Ironically, just weeks ago, Etsy announced tighter rules on misleading AI product photos and bot-generated listings. The marketplace still allows sellers to list work generated from their own prompts, as long as they disclose the use of AI to buyers.
Etsy says it uses sellers' content to spot fraud and help shoppers find products, and that it doesn't train models designed to copy artists' work. That said, the vague terms leave open just how its partners and vendors might use that same content...
The artists posting their goodbyes have decided it's worth walking away from that revenue source to keep their work from being used to train AI.
 
"In the wake of that reaction, Etsy vice president of customer operations James Ossman posted a separate update," writes PC Gamer, "saying it's not really like that at all... [T]hat 'we do not use your content to train AI models designed to copy your original work or replace your creativity.'"
"... we're enabling actions like translating your listing descriptions so buyers around the world can discover your items, or featuring your listing photos in Etsy marketing campaigns to showcase your creative work," Ossman wrote. "We also use your content to develop tools that make running your shop easier, like listing optimization tools or stats summaries that help you better understand your shop's performance. As always, your needs and feedback help guide how we build and improve these tools."
As for providing third-party access to that content, Ossman said that too is for "services and tools that can help you run your business," and promised that "when we partner with third parties, we do so thoughtfully to help protect your content and data."
That hasn't satisfied everyone. Ossman's commitment doesn't exist within the actual terms of service, which as seen above states only that Etsy and partners can "use Your Content to develop, train, test and improve artificial intelligence and machine learning technologies." Ossman also noted in his update that the terms "prohibit scraping Etsy or unauthorized use of content from Etsy to train AI models," but that (as far as I can tell) applies to Etsy users and unauthorized third-parties, not Etsy itself or those it licenses...
Others also complain that AI training should be opt-in rather than on by default — and worse, there's apparently no opt-out option available at all, so users who are opposed to having their work used for AI training for any reason are out of luck.
Ironically, the blowup over Etsy's embrace of AI training comes just weeks after it earned plaudits for banning AI-generated recipes and patterns, a move Ossman said was necessary to establish "trust" in the platform.

Localcode, open source agentic coding on your Mac with one command
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
22:18:48 UTC
07:18:48 KST

Agentic coding. Local models. On your Mac.
uv pip install localcode
Upgrade with uv pip install -U localcode.
cd your-project
localcode
To use the running model from another local app, open /server or run localcode api for its
OpenAI compatible endpoint. See the Local API guide.
- Reads and edits files in your project
- Runs your tests, builds, Git and shell commands inside the project on its own, and asks before touching anything outside it
- Searches code by name, content or structure
- Scaffolds and launches apps, then checks that they respond
- Remembers the task across messages
- Mac with Apple Silicon, macOS 13 or newer
- 16 GB unified memory or more
- Python 3.10 or newer
- Disk space for LocalCode and your chosen model (download sizes below)
Choose from 12 models and 169 quant options for coding, image input and typed decisions. Models download from Hugging Face. Downloads run in parallel and can resume if interrupted. Switch with /models. LocalCode recommends the most capable one that fits your Mac's memory and marks it with a star.
Speed: Gemma 4, Qwen 3.6, Muse Glimmer and Qwen 3.8 draft tokens with the vendor's own speculative drafter, which downloads with the model. The model server's GPU kernels ship precompiled, so the first start after an install is quicker.
What LocalCode adds on top of the stock model server to make local models faster and better at coding.
Speed
- Drafting: the vendor's own drafter guesses tokens ahead (about 2x faster replies on Gemma 4, Qwen 3.6, Qwen 3.8 and Muse Glimmer)
- Copy drafting: when the model rewrites a file it already read, it copies instead of generating (3.5 to 4x faster on rewrites)
- Prompt cache reuse: the system prompt stays the same every turn, so the model only reads what is new
- Warm-up: the first request's prompt is read while the model loads, so your first message starts sooner
- Precompiled GPU kernels: no half-minute shader compile on first start
- Threads matched to your Mac's performance cores
- Flash attention on by default
- Inkling 2: its experts run on the GPU (were silently on the CPU, about 5x slower), banded attention and a fused activation op halve its GPU memory and read prompts 20 to 30% faster
- Downloads run in parallel, resume if interrupted, and keep going while you use another model
Memory
- 8-bit KV cache by default: half the memory of full precision, so longer context fits
- Inkling 2 uses a 16-bit cache instead: its attention reads it directly, so long prompts read 1.4 to 1.8x faster and it needs 3.3 GB less memory at 128K context
- Context sized from what the server reports, so long chats compact before they run out of room
- The picker recommends the most capable model that fits your Mac
Quality
- Hidden thinking off by default, so answers come straight away; a model that gets stuck thinking is recovered instead of failing
- Plan panel: the model keeps a task list and is reminded to update it
- Stuck detection: a model that makes no progress, or keeps failing the same check, is nudged to change approach and then stopped so it reports what it has
- Every release is tested live on every model (load, reply, tool calls, images) before it ships
Inference is local. Three features use the network: model downloads, the web_search and web_fetch tools, and any MCP servers you add. See Network Boundary for the full list.
Powerful, personal AI should work for everyone, on any device, anywhere. That means running it locally. localcode is a first step.
To sponsor localcode, reach out.
See CONTRIBUTING.md.
Apache 2.0. See LICENSE.

Localcode, open source agentic coding on your Mac with one command
Unknown

2026-11-22

07:18:48 KST

Agentic coding. Local models. On your Mac.
uv pip install localcode
Upgrade with uv pip install -U localcode.
cd your-project
localcode
To use the running model from another local app, open /server or run localcode api for its
OpenAI compatible endpoint. See the Local API guide.
- Reads and edits files in your project
- Runs your tests, builds, Git and shell commands inside the project on its own, and asks before touching anything outside it
- Searches code by name, content or structure
- Scaffolds and launches apps, then checks that they respond
- Remembers the task across messages
- Mac with Apple Silicon, macOS 13 or newer
- 16 GB unified memory or more
- Python 3.10 or newer
- Disk space for LocalCode and your chosen model (download sizes below)
Choose from 12 models and 169 quant options for coding, image input and typed decisions. Models download from Hugging Face. Downloads run in parallel and can resume if interrupted. Switch with /models. LocalCode recommends the most capable one that fits your Mac's memory and marks it with a star.
Speed: Gemma 4, Qwen 3.6, Muse Glimmer and Qwen 3.8 draft tokens with the vendor's own speculative drafter, which downloads with the model. The model server's GPU kernels ship precompiled, so the first start after an install is quicker.
What LocalCode adds on top of the stock model server to make local models faster and better at coding.
Speed
- Drafting: the vendor's own drafter guesses tokens ahead (about 2x faster replies on Gemma 4, Qwen 3.6, Qwen 3.8 and Muse Glimmer)
- Copy drafting: when the model rewrites a file it already read, it copies instead of generating (3.5 to 4x faster on rewrites)
- Prompt cache reuse: the system prompt stays the same every turn, so the model only reads what is new
- Warm-up: the first request's prompt is read while the model loads, so your first message starts sooner
- Precompiled GPU kernels: no half-minute shader compile on first start
- Threads matched to your Mac's performance cores
- Flash attention on by default
- Inkling 2: its experts run on the GPU (were silently on the CPU, about 5x slower), banded attention and a fused activation op halve its GPU memory and read prompts 20 to 30% faster
- Downloads run in parallel, resume if interrupted, and keep going while you use another model
Memory
- 8-bit KV cache by default: half the memory of full precision, so longer context fits
- Inkling 2 uses a 16-bit cache instead: its attention reads it directly, so long prompts read 1.4 to 1.8x faster and it needs 3.3 GB less memory at 128K context
- Context sized from what the server reports, so long chats compact before they run out of room
- The picker recommends the most capable model that fits your Mac
Quality
- Hidden thinking off by default, so answers come straight away; a model that gets stuck thinking is recovered instead of failing
- Plan panel: the model keeps a task list and is reminded to update it
- Stuck detection: a model that makes no progress, or keeps failing the same check, is nudged to change approach and then stopped so it reports what it has
- Every release is tested live on every model (load, reply, tool calls, images) before it ships

‘You Can See Everything’ has made more than $100M worldwide
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
21:42:48 UTC
06:42:48 KST

“You Can See Everything,” Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s documentary about Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes, reached an impressive box office milestone this weekend, becoming one of the few documentaries to make more than $100 million worldwide.
Variety reports that almost all of the film’s $102.4 million box office has come from U.S. audiences, from whom it has grossed $93.4 million so far.
“You Can See Everything” will be opening in many more countries over the next few months — starting with Mexico, Brazil and Argentina on Nov. 25 and 26 — so its box office total could end up significantly higher.
And while the documentary was already an undeniable commercial success for indie studio A24 (especially with a reported budget of $7 million), it could now surpass the records set by the highest grossing documentaries of all time — “March of the Penguins” ($127 million globally) and “Fahrenheit 9/11” ($222 million globally).
Next up: Maybe the International Space Station? And the Oscars?
